894.24/1027
.Mr. F. E. Johnson of the M. W. Kellogg Company, Jersoy
City, New Jersey, telephoned me today and inquired whether
894.241
the Department would express an opinion on the sale by the
Kollogg Company to Mitsui and Company of plans for the con-
struotion of an oil-solvent plant in Japan. This plant,
larrels
when completed, would turn out 1,000
pounds per day of high-
grade lubricating oil. This lubricating oil is of a type
whioh could be used in all types of motors, motoroar as
well as airplane. Mr. Johnson explained that the Kellogg
Company was of the opinion that the sale of plans and
specifications for the construction of this type of plant
would not be contrary to the Government's policy with re-
spect to the delivery to certain countries of plans, plants,
manufacturing rights, or technical information required
for the production of high-quality aviation gasoline, but
F
the Kelloge Company did not like to go ahead with its
B
negotiations without knowing the Department's attitude.
He added that there were two other companies, tho Texas
Company

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
Company and the Max B. Miller Company, who were in a posi-
tion to sell technical information of this nature. I told
Mr. Johnson that I would be glad to refer this matter to
my superiors and I would call him back after consideration
had been given to this question.
I discussed the mater with Mr. Green, Mr. Hornbeck,
Mr. Hamilton, and Mr. Yost and consulted further with
Mr. Johnson by telephone. It was finally agreed that the
latter should write the Department setting forth in more
detail the exact character of the contract which his com-
pany contemplates concluding.
Hum
Harold W. Moseley

894.24/1028
Department of State
894.24
Pursuant to our exchange of telephone calls, I am sending you a
photostatic copy of the inquiry we received under date of May 27th, 1940,
from Mitsui & Company Limited covering the furnishing of a Propane JUIK.
Process Lubricating Oil Plant to handle 1,O00 barrels per day.
As I explained to you, there are three concerns in the United
States who are in a position to furnish plans and specifications for a
plant of this character, one being the M.E.K. Process, which is controlled
by The Texas Company; the JUIK Process, which has been developed and is owned
jointly by the Standard Oil Development Company, The Standard Oil Company of
Indiana, the Union Oil Company of California, and ourselves; and the Duosol
Process, which is controlled by Max B. Miller. Naturally, we have not in any
way taken this matter up with the other two companies involved.
Whereas this inquiry does not come under the moral embargo specifi-
cations, it is, of course, a plant that can make high quality lubricating oil
ideally suited for aviation engines.
We will not luote on furnishing plans and specifications and other
data necessary for the building of a plant of this character unt we have
heard from you.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
JUL 17 1940
ADVISER ON POLITICAL RELATIONS
July 11,
I share the view attributed by
implication to Judge Moore in CR's
memorandum to Mr. Green of July 3.
In my opinion, this Government's
polioy and procedure with regard to
"moral embargoes" and legislated
894.
embargoes are becoming more and more
confused, illogical, inconsistent
and futile, from point of view of
potential effectiveness in regard to
the joint objectives of keeping this
力
country out of war and ensuring its
security. Either our embargoes should
be made more extensive or the "moral
embargoes" should be dropped entirely
and the narrowest interpretation be
1
plaoed upon the legal provisions which
have been made for certain limited
embargoes.
PA/H:SKH:ZMK

DEPARTMENT OF ST
June 27, 1940
It appears regrottable that the sale of
such an important process in the control of
which the United States enjoys world leader-
ship should be made to the Japanese at this
time. However, a lubricating oil plant is
not within the soope of the present policy
relating to the delivery of plans, plants,
manufaoturing rights or technical information
required for the produotion of high quality
aviation gasoline. Furthermore, the provi-
sions of the new defense legislation apparently
place no restriotions on the release of such
information.

JUL 11 1940
MR. BERLE
In the administration of the moral embargoes, it has
894.24/102
894.74
been the uniform practice of the Department not to express
objection to any proposed transaction to which the moral
embargo, as publicly announced by the President and the
Secretary, was not applicable. This practice was adopted
after consultation among the officers of the Department
6
concerned. It has been felt that to express objection to
proposed transactions to which the moral embargoes were
not applicable would necessarily lead to penalizing those
companies which, on their own initiative, consulted the
Department in regard to such transactionsto the advantage
of their competitors acting in equally good faith on the
basis of the announced policy of the Department. Undoubted-
ly, this practice has resulted in a failure of the Depart-
ment to make adverse comments on proposed transactions
which, for one reason or another, we might heve prefer ed
to see dropped. Nevertheless in fairness to Amerfcan com-
panies, it has seemed wiser to adhere to it.
The

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.93)
121669
-2-
The attached letter to the M. W. Kellogg Company is
drafted in accordance with the precedents set by the practice
described above. It would seem that, in fairness to the
company, such a letter should be sent, as otherwise the
contract would probably go to one of the company's competitors
8
which in all good faith would not consider it necessary to
6
f
consult the Department in advance. There can be no question
2
but that a lubricating oil plant is not within the scope
of the announced policy of the Government relating to the
2
delivery of plans, plants, manufacturing rights, or technical
information required for the production of high-quality avi-
ation gasoline.
The only alternative would seem to be to extend the
moral embargo to include such transactions. This extension
was proposed in Mr. Yost's memorandum of March 2, 1940,
hereto attached, but the proposal did not meet with approval.
Joseph C. Green
O
CO:JCG:HJG

THE FAR EASTERN SITUATION
ULL1161940
mirability of: (1) Control of Exports of all War Materia
quipment: and (2) of Imposition of Additional Duties on Imports
DC
From Japan.
1. The powers granted to the President under the law to
expedite the national defense, approved July 2, 1940, permit the
exercise of large discretion to control exports of materials and
equipment in the interest of national secuity.
2. National secur1ty as affected by a vailability of materials
is a relative matter. If the actual or potential opponent has
large resources, our own requirements wiil be correspondingly in-
creased. If the opponent's resources sre reduced, our own arma-
ments may also be reduced.
3. Since Japan, Germany, and Italy are regarded as potential
opponents, it would appear desirable that we check dscreetly the
outflow from the United States of materials and equipment that
might increase the strength of those countries. Thereby some
lessening of their military and naval efficiency might be achieved,
8
or their financial resources in foreign exchange might be reduced
6
through t he necessity of buying in dearer markets, s ending more
4
for freight, and in some cases suffering &ctual deficiency in
essential supplies.
2
4. The possibility that the handicaps created by embargoes
might not alone be decisive is not an adequate argument gainst
export control, for in war an advantage or disadvantage t hat 1s
not very great in Itself may in addition to other factors have
an important effect. The anxiety and irritation produced in
Japan by the discussion of embargo  rop osals seems to indicate
that the enforcement of an embargo would heve a seriously em-
barrassing effect upon Japan.
5. On the face of it, the continued sale of wer materials
to Japan for use a gainst China, and a ainst American and British
interests in Asia seems absurd. It is recognized, however, that
the problem is not simple, since it would not be desirable to
provoke Japan to rash action against us or the British at this
tine. On the other hand, 1t is desirable not to permit Japan
to frignten us into a position in which our efforts at appease-
ment would serve only to strengthen her for further attacks upon
FILHI
our own and British, French, and Dutc interests in the Fer Fast.
6. It is desirable to consider whether there is a sound sfs
for the belief that leniency or yielding to Japan would produce
beneficial results. Vhat could be expected from Japan in return?
7. Japan would  robably give assurances that remaining
interests f the United States and Britain would be respected.

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Is there ground to believe that such assurances, even if given in
good faith by the Japanese government, would be respected by t he
Japanese army and navy?
8. Has the experience of the British, who made important
concessions to the Japanese in North China, been such as to en-
courage the belief that the Japanese army and navy would be so
satisfied with such concessions as we could make that they would
desist from further interference with our interests in the Far
East?
9. It is submitted that the weight of evidence tends to
the conclusion that the answer to points 7 and 8 must be in the
negative.
10. If concessions made to Japan by the British and our-
selves were inadequate to enable Japan to destroy t he Chinese
government fairly soon, it seems clear that the Japanese army and
navy would remain dissatisfied with American and British policy.
ll. If such conoessions as the closing of traffic with
China through Burma resulted in the breakdown of Chinese resistance,
Japanese resources in men and materials would then be aveilable
for other adventures, and increased pressure against American and
British interests in the Far Fast w ould be feasible for Japan.
12. If aggression against American and British interests were
nore feasible than :t present it seems thet the Japenese army and
navy would be more disposed, rather than less disposed, to attack
Britain and the United  tates in Asie. On the other hand, the
maintaining of a strong Chinese resistance or a Chinese victory
would seem likely to lessen Ja an's ower to act forcefully else-
where.
13. While the refusal of concessions to Japan and even the
imposition of economic handicaps to Japan's aggressive activities
seem to be indicated as desirable by the foregoing argument, it is
also desirable to & vid giving occasion, by too radical action,
for a violent reaction on Japan's part, in which emotion might
overcome considerations of rudence.
14. It is submitted that very gradual action might be taken,
no one stage being so serious as to cause : violent shock. The
following steps are suggested:
a. Inclusion of iron, steel, co,per, and scrap metel,
petroleum products, and motor vehicles in t he list of
articles for which export licenses are required, but
for the time being licenses to be issued for such ex-
ports to Japa n.
b. When Japan or the Japanese army takes some new un-
friendly action, licenses for export might be delayed,
without being actually refused. When representations

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
were made against such delays the idea might be tact-
fully suggested that perhaps the delays might be lessened
if the Japanese army were to behave in a more friendly
manner.
c. Duties might be imposed on certain Japanese products.
Our enormous expenditures for armament would make increas
duties on luxury goods like silk entirely reasonable.
Japan has gone much further by subjecting all imports to
a license system and has practically suspended the pur-
chase of certain articles.
d. One by one certain articles might be placed on a list
subject to absolute embargo as far as exports to the
aggressive powers are cpcerned. If scrap iron were em-
bargoed first, the desirability to Japan of not having
the list extended to include petroleum products, etc.
might suggest to the Japanese government the wisdom of
not reacting too violently.
15. The pover to take such steps is now in the hands of the
Administration. The Administrstion naturally will have to bear the
major share of the responsibility either for action or inaction.
Fither course will involve some risk, and the choice will not be
easy, since few are able to : e lict tnr future withc om, lete con-
fidence.
16. There have been in recent years conspicuous examples of
statesmen deciding to choose inaction or a peasement of aggressors
in Europe. The general opinion today, rightly or wrongly, is that
those statesmen were mistaxen and that they bear a large share of
responsibility for the distressin:  osition of France, Britain,
and the United States toiay.
17. As yet there seems t) r in ufficient evidence to justi-
fy the belief that a assive licy in a similar situation in the
Far East would lead to : o e satisfactory result than well-consider
positive action.
18. It 1s doubtrul wnether Ja an can sustain a much more ex-
tensive campaign tha at present, with much havifr consumption
than now of goods whicn :re obt: ined in large  irt fr m ourselves,
and in any case paid for f rom an inadequate stoc of foreign ex-
change, which trade with us nelps t @nserve. 'arlike o. erations
against Britain or the United  ta tes wo ld furtner de, lete for the
time being the avall ble stock of forelgn go pus and currency,
especially in view of the necessity of ee in: & c nciderable
reserve again:t the always possible con liet v it. !ussia.
19. The great ositive advantage to the democr tie po ers of
a rejuvenated China attached to their side, as a ain t a China
subject to the domination of either Japan or lussia, sho d not be
overlooked.

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.99)
20. Due consideration should be given to the probability that
any lessening of the Japanese preoccupation with China, which might
result from a decisive victory over Chiang K'ai-shek's government,
would cause Russia to be more concerned with her Far Fastern
defense and more dependent upon Germany in Furope, thus lessening
the effectiveness of Sir Stefford Cripps' efforts in Moscow, and
increasing the danger of Russian cooperation with Germany.
21. For the above reasons it is submitted thet the time has
come to consider seriously the cdoption of some such positive
program as is here suggested, supplemented, as soon as Congressional
action may permit, by liberal financial id to China.

Mr. Welles:
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
I had two calls at my house over the weekend by members
of the British Embassy, sent by the Ambassador to lay before
us considerations with respect to the President's proclama-
tion calling for the licensing of petroleum products and scrap.
The Ambassador has asked that these considerations be laid be-
894.24
fore you as soon as may be possible. I understand that he,
himself, is asking for an appointment to see you today.
According to telegrams received by the British Ambassador
from the Foreign Office, the British Government feels that the
874.
licensing proclamation has had a bad effect in Japan, and may
result in bringing matters to a head in the Far East. The
British Government point out the vital importance of high
2
octane gas in their present war effort, and state that they
S
E
FILED
are at present taking the whole output of high octane gas fron
ci
P
The Netherlands Indies. The British Government are in accord
1943
S
/KN
with our desire to prevent large deliveries of gasoline to
Japan at this time, but they feel that it is best not to bring
about a crisis at this time, nor to have to go back on measures
already taken. To sun up, the British are fearful that this
licensing measure may result in the Japanese taking steps to
insure possession and control of the petroleum products in
The Netherlands Indies.
Another

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
121671
-2 -
Another point brought up by the British Embassy is
with respect to the relationship between the British Govern-
ment and the American oil producers and exporters. The
British officials have been told by American producers with
some heat that the British Government is responsible for the
placing of restrictions on exports of certain petroleum
products from the United States in order to benefit through
the sale of these same products to the Jananese from other
sources, both British and Dutch.
The British officials are
extremely apprehensive whether the picture presented as a
result of the President's proclamation will not seem to
justify the accusation now being made by the American oil
companies along those lines. The British make the statement
that the matter of their relationship with the American oil
companies is of primary importance to them in the carrying
out of the war.
They state that 1f the American oil companies
really believe that the British have instigated the President's
action for the purpose of benefiting from the restriction of
the sale of American products, the oil companies can greatly
hamper British action in defense of the British Isles, not
to speak of matters relating to the Far East and other areas.
The British Enbassy is particularly anxious that we take
steps to make known to the American oil companies, both on
the Atlantic and the Pacific seaboards that this action of
the President was taken entirely without any relationship to
the British, and most certainly not at their inspiration or
instigation,

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
121672
-3 -
instigation, and that it is entirely based upon a strictly
American policy. (This suggestion appears reasonable to me:
the oil companies which will be represented at your forth-
coming meeting regarding the Spanish Atlantic area could be
informed at that time along these lines, and, if you approve
of so doing, I could communicate with Mr. Grady, now in San
Francisco, and have him drop that word to the oil companies
of the Pacific coast, who, no doubt, will all be represented
at the meeting of the National Trade Council which Mr. Grady
is attending.)
Another matter the British were concerned with was the
question of aviation gasoline and aviation lubricating oil
which they now draw from the United States for their require-
ments in Great Britain. I told Mr. Butler, Counselor of the
Embassy, both Saturday and yesterday, that I could assure
him that there was absolutely no intention whatever of
restricting supplies of this kind in the case of British re-
quirements, and that as licenses will be ranted for countries
in this hemisphere, they could always draw as much as they
requlred through their Canadian companles, and that if any
difficulties arose in re ard to t is question, we were ready
to discuss them as they came un.
I cannot too strongly point out the extreme importance
the British Embassy give to the points raised above and it
appears that (1) the licensing system would have most drastic
results in the Far East, and (2) that it would be creating
an extremely adverse disposition on the part of the American
o11

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.95)
121673
-4 -
oil companies toward the British Government and would inter-
fere greatly with the present British war effort.
James Cfement Dunn.

1—1493
Mr. James A. Moffett telephoned from New York and
was referred to me by the Secretary's office. Mr. Mof-
fett said that two high officials of one of the oil
companies with which he is connected had, while passing
through Japan recently, received and booked orders of
substantial quantities of gasoline and oil products for
delivery in Thailand. Mr. Moffett said that the commodi-
ties ordered included aviation gasoline. He said that
F
the orders had been placed by Japanese but repeated that
ω
the commodities were to be delivered in Thailand.
AUG 80 1940
Mr. Moffett referred to the fact that the Thai
E
Government had purchased a number of naval vessels in
Japan, and he said that the Japanese activity directed
toward the shipment to Thailand of large quantities of
oil produots raised the question in his mind whether
Japan

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Japan might not be endeavoring to store up in Thailand
large quantities of oil products which might be used in
Japanese military and naval operations southward, possi-
bly against Singapore, et cetera.
In reply to my inquiry, Mr. Moffett said that all
these sales were being made on a strictly cash basis.
He said that a recent development was that the
Japanese were now putting pressure on one of his Cali-
fornia subsidiaries to sell a large quantity of tetra-
ethyl lead to Japan. Mr. Moffett was not clear whether
the tetraethyl lead was to be delivered to Japan or to
Thailand. He said that the California subsidiary was
inclined to make the sale. He said that a complicating
factor was that the British subsidiary of the Ethyl
Corporation was willing to sell tetraethyl lead to
Japan in order to get foreign exchange.
Mr. Moffett said that, if we wished to discourage
the sale of the tetraethyl lead to Japan, it would be
advisable that we get in touch with Mr. Webb, head of
the Ethyl Corporation, and make known to him our attitude
and suggest to him that he get in touch with the British
subsidiary with a view to the discouraging of the sale by
the British subsidiary oftetraethyl lead to Japan.
I told Mr. Moffett that I would like to talk this
matter over with some of my associates, after which I
might care to get in touch with him again by telephone.
(H1s

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
(His private telephone is Murray Hill 9399l.)
I told Mr. Moffett that we appreciated very much
his thoughtfulness in bringing this information to our
attention.
Comment: Neither tetraethyl lead nor aviation
gasoline is included within any existing moral embargo
relating to exports, nor has either commodity been
included within the list of commodities at present
regulated by the National Defense Act.

Dear Sir:
Confirming my telephone conversation with Mr. H. W. Mosley, we
are requested to offer the design and construction of a thermal cracking
894.24/1041
plant for a Japanese refinery for the manufacture of gasoline which will be
the equivalent of first quality American motor fuel without the addition of
tetraethyl lead.
The raw materlal or charging stock for the unit would be a gas
oil derived from California crude.
894.24
The cracking process under consideration, termed "Polyform", is
a modern development in the art and produces gasoline of say 75 octane motor
method without lead, which in general can be stated to be the equivalent of
the best house brand leaded motor gasolines as retailed by the major companies.
While it nas not been discussed, nor to our information contem-
plated, it is possiole by re-processing this gasoline in the same unit, to
obtain a product of 82 octane number, which product by acid treating will
produce a final product of about 78 octane number with an acid heat of 15,
which material is the equivalent of specially selected natural gasolines and
can be used as a filler, or oase stock for eviation gasoline.
N0918194
Aviation gasoline is a mixture of approximately 50% base stok
plus 50% iso-octane plus tetraethyl lead.
What the Japanese use in their airplanes I do not know, but 
doubtedly they get along at lessened efficiency with a poorer product than
our American standarc aviation fuels.
The point Is tnat waile this plant uroduces a motor fuel, the
product is quite superior to the ususl run of crackint nlants and, of course,
the better the quality of the produet the closer it approaches the grade
suitable for airplane enrines.
F/B
Kindly advise if there is oujection to building this unit for
Japan.
I will be glad to furnish additional information or can meet
your technical representative if you desire.
Yours very truly,
THE LUMMUS COLIPANY

=== Page 51 === (confidence 0.99)
JOSEPH A. HARWOOD
Lunenburg, Mass.
August 27, 1940
Hon. Joseph E. Casey, M.C.
Washington, D. C.
Dear Congreasman Casey:
Although I am and have long been in hearty accord
with President Roosevelt's and Seoretary Hull's foreign
policy on nearly all points, there 1s one which I do
not understand. This 1s the apparent reluctance to crack down
on Japan's purchases of sorap 1ron and aviation gasoline in
the United States.
Why do we jeopardize our owm defense by supplying
Japan, our potential enemy, with the means to establish
her strangle-hold on China? For the past ten days Japan
has renewed her massacres of the civilian populace of
Chungking, using undoubtedly for that purpose the very
gasoline and metals which we have sold her. Is it good
policy on our part to contribute to the weakening of our
potential ally, China, whose continued resistance is vital
to our own defense in the Paoific?
The administration is now authorized to withhold
l1oenses for shipments of gasoline and scrap metal to
Japan, and yet within two weeks I have seen 1n"The Boston
Herala" Notice of the granting of a license to a ship
loaced with scrao iron to sa1l to Japan. Why 1s this
allowed? Is the administration trying to "appease"
Japan? Is the President afraid thot Janan, if we shut
off her gas and metal, will seize the Dutch East Indies?
Anything which you can find out, that will throw
light on this distressing situation, and anything which
you can do to stop 1t, will be deeply appreciated.
Two things more: (1) I am heartily in favor of
selective service from ages eighteen to sixty-four.
The House committee on military affairs has set a com-
mendable examole to the Senate.
(2) The old destroyers should be given or sold to
England, and guickly.
Yours respeotfully,
JOSEPH A. HARWOOD

Recent inteneified bombings of Chungking, China, the
victims of whioh are almost wholly civilians, remind
us that the United States 1s still supplying Japan w1th

bombings possible.

Can you find out why the Administration 1s not acting
more effectively? In the eyes of an overwhelming part
of our people effective action 1s long overdue.

Dear Senator,
Just in case you have not been informed of the follow
up of the "sorap iron to Japan" matter, which you heard
from me about before, I wish to state that, sorap iron
being taboo, now, new molten metal is being shipped direct
from the Tacoma smelter to replace the sorap 1ron, to
Japan. You did as well as you could about the scrap iron
shipments. I hope it will be possible to stop the latter
also.
I have read what the magazine "Technooraoy" says about
consoription. I understand a copy has been sent all sena-
tors. I believe their plan excellant. I believe volun-
tary enlistment might be possible if our men were guarranted
that they were to protect the continent of North Amerioa,
with no possibility of being sent to any other continent to
be involved in other naticns scraps or to protect individuals
or comaany who persist in living or gathering the wealth
of another continent at the risk of involving us in a war
with that nation. Let those who live or operate in other
countries do so at their own risk as citizens of that
country.
Also why guarantee a profit in case of war to industry?
If profit is to be guaranteed industry 1t should also be
guaranteed to the men. A man's life 1s so much more valuable.
Those who go must sacrifice in money matters as well as
everything else. Conscrip wealth also and there will be
less chance that we go to war.

THIS AGREEMENT, made and entered into this 19th day
of July, 1928, by and between UNIVERSAL OIL PRODUCTS COM-
PANY, a Corporation duly organized and existing under and
by virtue of the laws of the State of South Dakota, having
1ts principal office in the City of Chicago, State of
Illinois, United States of America, party of the first
part, and hereinafter referred to as "UNIVERSAL," and
HISASHI SEKIGUCHI, of Tokyo, Japan, party of the second
part, hereinafter referred to as "SEKIGUCHI";
W I T N E S S E T H :
WHEREAS SEKIGUCHI is desirous of acquiring for himself

relating to cracking and treating petroleum oil in Japan, now

pending applications and inventions covered thereby in Japan
on the following terms and conditions, it being understood
and agreed that Japan in this agreement shall be inclusive
of its territories Korea, Formosa, southern half of Sakhalin,
the leased territory of Kwantung and the portion of Southern
Manchuria specifically recognized by the United States as be-
longing to Japan. It is agreed by both parties hereto:

Mr. Morishima, Counselor of the Japanese Embassy:
called this afternoon at his request. He said that the
Japanese Ambassador had asked him to express appreoiation
of the prompt and favorable action taken by the Department
in reference to the contract of 1928 between the Universal
Oil Products Company and the Japan Gasoline Company.
Mr. Morishima continued that he referred to the fact that
yesterday Mr. Berle had informed the Japanese Ambasador
that the Department was prepared to recommend to the
C
Universal Oil Products Company that it proceed with fulfil-
ment of its 192s contract, provided that the Japan Gasoline
Company would withdraw the legal proceedings instituted
against the Universal Oil Products Company on the basis of
the 1928 contract. Mr. Morishima said that following
Mr.
F/N

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
Mr.Berle's conversation with the Japanese Ambassador, he
(Mr. Morishima) had gotten in touch by telephone with
Mr. Saneyoshi, President of the Japan Gasoline Company;
that Mr. Saneyoshi had come to Was ington today for
further discussion of the matter; that although Mr. Sane-
yoshi had been somewhat reluctant to agree to withdraw
legal proceedings he had finally expressed his willingness
to do so. Mr. Morishima said that he wished to explain
one technical point to me which had been explained to him
by Mr. Saneyoshi; Mr. Saneyoshi contended that the poly-
merization process was covered by the l928 contract whereas
Mr. Halle contended that this process was not included in
the 1928 contract but was included in the 1938 contract.
Mr. Morishima said that the patent to the polymerization
process had already been turned over by Mr. Halle's company
to the Japan Gasoline Company. Mr. Morishima said further
that Mr. Saneyoshi had agreed to drop the legal proceedings
based on the 1928 contract and felt that the question
whether the polymerization process was included in the 1928
contract should be left for discussion and decision by
the Japanese company and by the American company.
I said that while I could not make any definitive
comment I might observe that should there develop any
question of haggling over details the whole question might
become very complicated.
Mr.

=== Page 83 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Mr. Morishima said that Mr. Saneyoshi was returning
to New York tonight; and that Mr. Saneyoshi would probably
get in touch with Mr. Halle. Mr. Morishima said that he
expected that Mr. Halle would come to Washington tomorrow
or the next day where, according to what Mr. Berle had
said to the Ambassador, Mr. Berle would explain to Mr. Halle
the Department's decision in reference to the 1928 con-
tract.
With regard to the 1938 contract, Mr. Morishima
said that Mr. Berle had informed the Ambassador that
this matter presented difficulties; and that it would
be given further study by the Department. Mr. Morishima
indicated that, in view of Mr. Berle's forthcoming de-
parture for Habana, he did not expect that any decision
in regard to the 1938 contract would be reached by the
Department in the near future.
Mr. Morishima said that he wished to refer to the
fact that in Mr. Saneyoshi's opinion the 1938 contract
inoluded not only the process for aviation gasoline but
processes and plans for a number of other commodities
and that Mr. Saneyoshi believed that a distinction could
be drawn between the process for aviation gasoline and
the processes for other commodities. Mr. Morishima said
that Mr. Saneyoshi would be willin, provided a compromise
could be arrived at whereunder his company would be denied
the

=== Page 84 === (confidence 0.96)
-4-
the use of the process for aviation gasoline but would
be supplied with the other processes, to consider with-
drawing the legal proceedings instituted by him on the
basis of the 1938 contract.
I repeated that I was not in position to make any
definitive comments. I said that I would be glad to
make record of what Mr. Morishima had said and to bring
it to the attention of other officers of the Department.
Mr. Morishima asked whether the Embassy might ex-
pect to receive a written reply to the written commu-
nications which the Japanese Ambassador had left with
Mr. Berle on July 9. I replied that I would prefer to
leave that question in abeyance in as much as it would
probably be necessary for us to have a number of
further conversations with Mr. Morishima in regard to
this whole matter.

Dear Mr. Hornbeck:
Department of State
I enclose a copy of a memorandum of what seems
to me a desirable course of action in the Far Fast,
which I have submitted to the President, the Secre-
taries of State, Var, Navy, and Treasury, to Senator
894.24/1055
Pittman, end to Congressman Sol Bloom. I also en-
close copies of the covering letters which I sent
to the President and Secretary of State.
I hope you will find some merit in my argument.
You may be interested to learn that Mr. Kurt Bloch
has come to the conclusion that an embargo on scrap
iron would be useful after all. This conclusion is
due to the information he has received that about
the middle of June the Japanese government decided
to subsidize scrap iron imports. If this news is
correct or if any kind of subsidy is bein given to
steel producers in Japan, it would suggest that a
scrap iron embargo would have a significant effect.
Yours sincerely,
loger S. Ereeme

=== Page 91 === (confidence 0.98)
THE FAR EASTERN SITUATION
allty of: (1) Control of Exports of all War Materials and
att end (2) of Imposition of Additional Duties on Imports
1. The President has discretion under the act approved July
2, 1940, to control exports of war materials in the interest of
national security.
Reduction of supplies to actual or potential enemies is
as important for security as are adequate supplies for our own use.
Since Japan, Germany, and Italy are potential opponents,
we should gain by checking our exports of war materials or equip-
ment to those countries. This might cause actual deficiency in
their supplies or by raising prices elsewhere reduce their stock
of foreign exchange.
The fact that embargoes alone may not be decisive is not
an adequate argument against them, since added to other factors
their effects may be important. Japanese dreed of an embargo
suggests that it would have some value to us.
Would leniency or ylelding to Japan produce good results?
Japan would probably give assurances of respect for cer-
tain interests of ours in the Far East. Past experience suggests
that the Japanese army and navy would disregard such obligations
at will.
The British have made 1mportant concessions to Japan in
North China without gaining any improvement in relations. More
severe pressure on Britain has followed those concessions.
If further concessions  proved inadequate to cause a Chinese
collapse the Japanese would continue t, be dissatisfied with British
and American policy.
V If Chinese resistance ended, Japan would be able to pro-
ceed more vigorously again t. other British and American interests in
East Asia, and would probably do so with little delay.
The above argument seems to indicate the wisdom of refusing
to y1eld to Japan, and the desirability of wi thholding further
economic aid by cheoking exports of war naterlals and by reducing
Japan's stock of foreign exchange through limitation of m orts from
Japan. Continuance of such aid would 'ncrease the danger ageinst
which we nave to arm.
2. It is desirable to av id, 1f possible, violent reaction in
Japan. A gradual program, to be modified according to drcumstances,
might prevent that result. The following steps are tentatively
suggested:
a. Inclusion of iron, s teel, copper and sera, metal,
petroleum products, and motor vehicles and parts in the list
of articles for which export llcenses are required, but con-

=== Page 92 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
timuance for a time of the issuance of licenses for such
exports, except scrap iron and steel, the price of whioh has
been artificially enhanced by foreign demands for war purposes
Serap iron lends itself well to immediate action since an
embargo on such exports is desired by independent steel pro-
ducers as well as by the majority of disinterested American
citizens.
b. If Japan continues unfriendly or takes some new hostil
action, licenses for export for other articles may first be
delayed and later if necessary refused, one by one or several
at a time as circumstances may make desirable.
c. New or increased duties night be imposed on Japanese
products, especially luxury goods such as silk. Jep an has
gone much further by controlling all imports and suspending
or reducing pruchase of many of our products.
3. The power and responsibility for actlon now rests with the
Administration. Either action r inaction involves risks.
4. Recent experience seems to justify the common belief that
in Europe inaction and attempted appeasement of aggressors proved
the more dangerous course.  There is little evidence if any that a
passive policy in the Far Fast would bring better results.
5. It 1s doubtful whether Japan can sustain & much more ex-
tensive campaim than at present with larger consumption of material
bought from abroad than the present operations in China require,
especially since constant anxiety about possible conflict with
Russia compels the maintenance of large reserves for that con-
tingenoy. The Japanese combine gresl. caution before action with
courage in actual combat.
6. The great positive advantage  the democratio powers of
a rejuvenated China attached to thei: side, as against a China
dominated by Japan or Russia should not be overlooked.
7. A decisive Japanese victory over China would cause Russia
to be more concerned about her Far Fastern defense and would there-
fore make her weaker in Europe and more Lnclined to c operate there
with Germany.
8. The proposed suspension o affic througn Burma indicates
an urgent need for strengthenint bcs orale by some independent
action of cur own.
9. For the above reasons I  s subuitted that the time has
come for some such positive action s s here suggested, to be
supplemented as soon as ssible by iberal financlal aid to China.

Dear Mr. Greene:
Thank you very much for your letter of yesterday and the
enclosed check.
The remark in your first criticel par: greoh as to the mar-
ginal importance of the reduced Amcrican supplies bought by
Japan, 1s, I admit, completely in order, insofar as general
considerations are concerned. On the other hand, while such
marginal importance may eri t, it mey not be vital at all.
Moreover, I feel that, insofar as such importations were
really necessary from a technological point of view, Japan would
probably get hold of them any ay. Given the present position,
I think such - possibly "illegai" - transshipments via, e.s.,
the Netherlands Indies and French Tndochina could scarcely be
completely stopped, and cert: inly not before a considerable
lapse of time. - This, e.s., would be especially t rue of motor
equipment, where you can speek from experience.
As to Chilean copper, you must not overlook that Japan main-
tains copper smelters end refineries of its own which could be
used, if the American supply  ere cut off. This would, of course,
necessitate a somewhat lar er outlay in cash, but scarcely one
worth stressing.
As to gasoline, I am afraid my point is now, after the
French armistice, much stronger than before. Fven at the time
of writing, however, the international price structure of the
gasoline market left little room for your tentative argument.
As to high octane gas, you are certainly right; however,
it is now less than ever important for Japan, as the distance
Ichang-Chungking could be flown easily with cheaper motor fuel.
As to lubricating oils, I think the sharp upturn in Japanese
purchases, during a period of reduced industrial activity, fully
justified my conclusion as to the accumulation of stocks.

=== Page 98 === (confidence 0.99)
Japanese sensitiveness on the subject of the embargo is
tainly still in existence. I mentioned the machine tool em-
this embargo had already been adopted. However, consideration
item by item would, I think, lead necessarily to the same con-
clusion at which I have arrived in my paper, namely that the
embargo was now of presumably lesser incidence than at any
previous time.
As things have meanwhile developed, there is, however, one
qualification. This cualification applies to scrap iron. Toward
the middle of June, the Japanese Government has decided to sub-
sidize scrap iron imports, i.e. to pay special subsidies to
Japanese steel makers, in order to remove the handicap constituted
by excessive - from the Japanese point of view - A merican
scrap iron prices. I would, therefore, now once more be strongly
in favor of agitation for a scrap iron embargo or license scheme
designed to curb Japanese purchases.
As to the import duty on raw silk, I wish to stress agein
that now there is no need for a discriminatory duty on Japanese
raw silk; a general revenue duty, within the generel program of
raising adequate revenue for defense, would do the same service,
and I should be very glad, indeed, to see your Committee go
against the Treasury by advocting its adoption in Congress. As
to the popular reaction in Japan, I presume you are awere of the
fact that now Japan consumes more silk t han the United States,
and the bulk of Japanese raw silk is now grown for the domestic
market, I trust the strength of the farmers' movement in Japa n
to induce the government to pay subsidies on silk exports. More-
over, I do not think Japanese snesitiveness matters very much now
anyhow. The American problem is hov best to weaken Japan.
I cannot see that I have put any undue stress on Japanese
reserve stocks. On the contrary, I felt Mrs. Schumpeteris stress
on stocks about which little can be known was unwarranted. The
case of lubricating oils is quite different.
By the way, did you realize that the new embargo on scrap
rubber exports is not at all unimportant for Japanese-American
trade relations?
As to my previous position with regard to the embargo against
Japan, I have never been in favor of more than the embargo on
scrap iron. Up to the outbreak of the European war, I felt the
embargo would be an empty gesture. Afterwards, there were about
6 months when it could have been a very effective means of
pressure. Now, this period belongs to the regretted past. I
never have liked to parade this disagreement with my friends, so
it may have escaped your attention easily; but you will find it
even in my discussion with Mrs. Schumpeter in PACIFIC AFFAIRS.
While I woul d not feel ashamed of having changed my views with
changing conditions, they have actually so far fitted in pretty
well with them; frankly, I wish they had fitted less.
With best regards,
Yours very sincerely,
KURT BLOCH

CEORCE BURRIE, AMERICAN, CHIEF MECHANIC OF DOMINICAN AIR FORCE, RECEIVES OV
FROM JAPANESE TO RE-EXPORT AIR ECUIPMENT TO JAPAN. OFFER MADE BY SAKIE MISO
THROUGH LOCAL JAPANESE NAMED MITZUTANI. MANJO SAID TO BE SOUNDING OTHER L. A.
COUNTRIES ON SAME PROPOSITION.
CONFIDENTIAL
Mr. EORGk BURRIk, American, Chier Mechanic of the Dominican Air Foroe whe
is under eontract to Curtiss-right but who is paid by the Dominican Govermment,
has been heard to say that he had been made a proposition by the Japanese
Gevermment recently which if he had accepted "would have placed him on easy
street."
894.24/1063
From an investigation conducted by the Naval Attaché it has been learned
that a Japanese from Cuba who gave his name as NAnjo was introduced to Mr.
BURRIE by another Japanese named KTYOSHI MITZUTANI who has besn resident in
Ciudad Trujillo for two years and who is the local official Delegate of the Jap-
anese Federation of Exporters and Importers in Latin America. NAnJo showed
interest in the Dominican Air Force and its equipment. Three weeks ago,
MITZUTANI approached BURRIE with a proposition to import planes and parts for
re-shipment on the N. Y. K. Line to Japan. According to Mr. BURRIE, MITZUTANI
was aoting for NAnjo who holds diplomatic rank and who visits other Latin
American countries with the purpose of inducing these latter countries to sell
old air equipment or to import other equipment for re-shipment to Japan.
According to records maintained in this office, a SAkNE MANJO, Japanese,
age 40, married, travelling under a diplomatic pass-port arrived on 20 February,
1940, in the Dominican Republic via PAA from Santiago, Cuba. The Attach6 has
no record of his departure by PAA but considers it probable that MAnJO could
have lert the country by other means without the Attache's knowledge. As far
as is knowm, MANJO is not here now. It is assumed that this MANJO is the same
NANJO deseribed by Mr. BURRIE. Another Japanese with a similar name also
arrived in the Dominican Republic from Cuba on 28 March and departed for Miami
on 31 Marah. This 1s SABURA NANGo, 61, married, and who was also travelling
under a diplomatic pass-port.
Although BURRIE has a contract with Curtiss-#right to sell planes and
equipmont, it is not clear how the Japanese hoped for the success of such a
scheme as the position BURRIE holds in the Dominican Air Force is relatively
minor. Its success would have depended on connivance with high Dominican
officials. It is not known to the Attaché whether any of these has been
approached.

SO MUCH WE DID NOT BRING
By John Russell McCarthy
E
NRico stared at the man's feet on the trail
and at his hands bruising the rain-fresh sage.
"Surely I know him." Enrico said. "for he
has been I since there was no world at all:
yet how can I know a fellow so full of so much
we did not bring? Through his nose he has stolen a day-high
mountain and carries it somewhere: April grasses,
morning sumac, cedar after rain.
His eyes have swallowed a valley for his heart.
Time has not fingers sharp nor long enough
to dig the pink arbutus from his youth.
"He carries on the trail the voices of men dead,
of women dead, of children dead. In him
where his back bends are the screams of captured cities;
in his upturned eyes is the hope of forgotten nations.
His hand clenches his own years' broken stem.
Surely I know him," Enrico said, staring
at the man's slow steady fect upon the trail.

August 24, 1940.
mn wMr. Welles:
I agree with practically everything that is said
by Mr. Smith in the article ("We Can Stop Japan", by
Robert Aura Smith, The American Mercury, September 1940,
pp. 7-15) hereunder which he has brought to your atten-
tion. I question only one point.
In terms of capacity, this country and Great Britain
could have "stopped" Japan in 193l; this country and Great
Britain could have "stopped" Japan in 1937; and this
country could, the situation in Europe and the situation
in the Far East being what they are today, "stop" Japan
now.
I do not believe that, with determined and consistent
and sustained effort, skillrully handled, on this Govern-
ment's part, in the application of pressures, there need
be any real danger of Japan's makin; war on us or compelling
us to make war on her.
Exertion by the United States of new and ever greater
material pressures upon Japan might precipitate action by
Japan in the direction of movins into Indochina and/or of
"seizing" the Netherland Jast niies. If, with or without
such pressure, Japan were to tate suen action, 1 myself
would not be greatly perturbed by that or those developments--
for reasons which will be given intre.
Japan

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Japan will decide, Japan will move or not move
against or into French Indochina and/or the Netherland
East Indies on the basis of estimates by the Japanese
military element of potential "force versus obstacle".
Japan's leaders are at present testing out the situation,
trying to get as far as possible by diplomatic maneuverings
backed by threats of force. If they knew (or thought)
that the French and/or the Dutch would oppose then with
force, they would proceed very cautiously. If they know
(or think) that they can seize either or both of these
colonial areas without havin to fiht and therefore with-
out having to send large forces--including inportant naval
vessels--and to take new risks in the process, they will
probably soon move against one and expect later to move
against the other. -- They have already made an estimate
that the United States will not interpose with armed force;
they do not forget that while our fleet is in the Pacific
we might do that; they are prepared to risk small, new
expeditionary forces; they would hesitate to risi: sending
out a large such force--especially to the Netherland East
Indies (the doing of which would render then highly vulnerable
to an interference--if and when--by the United States).
The

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
The Japanese are tightly enmeshed in the situation
which they have produced in China. They cannot at present
get themselves disentangled from that situation. Japan
is capable of a separate effort in addition, but only a
comparatively small such effort. The Japanese army and
navy can let the China situation "ride" along, cut to
some extent their expenditures there, strike a sudden blow
somewhere else, and hope to profit thereby. They can risk
entering Indochina and pernaps later having to get out,
with an explanation that they had simply moved in tempo-
rarily to be of assistance while France was unable to ad-
minister that colony. They would have to take a greater
risk were they to seize the Netherland East Indies. They
may persuade themselves tnat they are capable of taking
that greater risk and the ensuin; risks which the having
taken of it might involve. But they are not equipped to
take on a war with the Uhite  :t tes et thi time: they
hove ne denire ror war with the United States; they have
no intention of riskin: e war with the United States; and
they will not o far in the field of &ctions the taking of
which by then they would estimt te likely to bring on such
a war. They oy  ake miseelcul: ticns, but they will not
deliberately act witi out havint carnestly and intensively
calculated.
were

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
Were the Japanese--with or without the impulse of
pressures by the United States from the effects of which
they would seek an escape, and with or without the desire
to show us that the harder we press the more independently
they will act--to go into Indochina in force and/or to move
toward seizure of the Netherland East Indies, I myself
would, as stated above, not be greatly perturbed. -- Naval
and military movements by Japan into and across Indochina
would amount in substance and in effect to a further extend-
ing of Japan's already over-extended lines in the prosecution
of her military operations against China. It would not im-
prove her strategic position in those operations. It would
increase the cost to her of those operations. It would add
to her difficulties from several points of view. It would
sive her a new enemy (France and the people of French Indo-
china). It would produce evidence additional to that which
the world already has of Japan's policy of aggression and
of the fact that there are no definite limits to her objec-
tives of acquisiticn (by aggrension). It would render the
Jujanese somewhat more a prehersive than now regarding pos-
sible action in the future by the United States. It would
render then more apprehensive re arding possible action by
the Soviet Union. It would bring nearer the day and make
greater the certainty--if the battle of ingland goes against
the Germans--of a liquication of Japan's imperialistic program.
Were

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
Were the Japanese in the near future, either before or
after going into French Indochina, to move against the
Netherland East Indies, many of the results outlined in the
preceding paragraph and several other important results also
would flow therefrom. In the latter group would be the fact
that Japan's naval lines would have been extended to a point
three thousand miles fron the principal home bases and that
by that process Japan would have developed a naval flank open
to attack from liawaii and from Singapore while at the same
time exposing Japan proper as regards physical security. The
British would be infuriated; also the Dutch. The people of
the United States would be tremendously influenced in the
direction of clarity of vision regarding present-day Japan,
and a clearer realization that this Japan is a public nuisance
and a real menace to the security of the United States and to
the cause of peace and order within the world. By this move,
also, the day of liquidation and of reorientation of Japan's
policies would have been brouht reerer.
So long as the Japanese pursue the policy to which they
have been conumitted increasingly durin: the last decade, and
until they have been ". topped" sonewhere by obstacles which
cause tnem to sit down and refleet upon and become convinced
of the ultimate futility of that policy, the prospect of
there havin: to be a "showdown" inereases; and the longer
the evil day is put off, the more costlv may be the process
by

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.97)
- 6-
by which other countries, especially the United States,
will have to interpose the obstacle. If Japan will but
burst herself, by too many and too great adventurings,
the day of a military "showdown" between her and the United
States may never need come.
All said and done, Japan is comparatively weak today:
her army is "out"--tied up in China; her navy is intact and
free but is not prepared for any double involvement; her
economy is under strain. Further adventurings, if under-
taken, will further weaten her. Occupation of Indochina
would not make her stronger. Seizure of the Netherland East
Indies would increase the quantity and variety of resources
at her disposal but weuld not immediately make her stronger.
Japan is gambling. If she makes moves into Indochina
or e gainst the Hetherland East Inaies now, she will be
weaker vis-à-vis the world, especiully the United States,
tomorrow ti.an se is today.
It is in the light of these considerations that I feel
that we should not be greetly infiuencen, in determinin: our
courses, pesitive or ne ative, by the consideration that
rirm or etron: uction on our p rt might push Japan inte ad-
venturings in the directions indicnted. Were we fellowing
Machiavellian prineiples, it ii it well be contended that we
should take certain steps for the delib rate purpose of
pushing Jaran inte sueh venturings.

WORLD EXPORTS OF WAR MATERIALS TO JAPAN FOR
THE THREE YEARS, 1937 - 1939
(A Statistical Summary)
War material exports to Japan from some twenty-eight countries or colonial
1
dependencies totaled 292,075,000 in 1939. This compares with totals of
306,230,000 in 1938 and $316,931,000 1n 1937. Wh1le last year's shipments
represent a decline in value of 4.6 per cent from 1938, and 7.8 per cent from
1937, they contimued to constitute, as in 1938, about 50 per cent of Japan's
total import trade, as against 32 per cent in 1937. Conversely, raw materials
essential for the export industries, as well as all other merchandise formerly
imported for domestic peace-time consumption, were reduced to 50 per cent of the
total imports in the last two years, whereas in l937 such commodities still com-
2
prised about 68 per cent of all of Japan's imports.
I. Commodity Analysis of War Material Shipments
About 95 per cent of the aggregate total value of war materials shipped to
Japan in 1939 can be accounted for by fifteen leading 1tems out of a total of
twenty-five, as shown in Table I below. This group of commodities was of sub-
stantially the same relative importance, compared with the aggregate total ship-
ments, as in 1938 and 1937.
By combining all iron materials, namely, iron ore, ferro-alloys, scrap iron,
and other iron and steel semi-munufactures, it is possible to indicate the four
major commodity groups among those listed in the table below. In Table II, it
can be seen that in 1939 petroleum and products, valued at $71, 220,000, consti-
tuted the largest single category among these groups, representing nearly one-
fourth of the total value of war material shipments to Japan. Iron materials,
valued at 62,894,000, ranked second in importance, or about one-fifth of the
total. The two 1tems taken together comprised 45.9 per cent of the total war
1 A few countries have necessarily been excluded from this survey because of
lack of data. It is believed, however, that such exclusions do not mater-
ially affect the aggregate total value of war material shipments.
See Appendix Table 10.

The most striking change in shipments sinoe 1938 is exhibited by aircraft
and parts, whioh dropped in value from $22,693,000 in 1938 to about one-fourth
3
that amount, or $5,746,000, in 1939.
This can be attributed for the most part
to the "moral embargo" placed on shipments of airplanes and airplane engines from
the United States, and to some extent to the cessation of shipments from Germany
soon after the outbreak of the European war. Automobiles, parts and accessories
also experienced a significant decrease, dropping from $18,635,000 in 1938 to
9,109, 000 in 1939. In view of the fact that during the period preceding 1939,
Japan's purchases of automotive equipment were maintained at a high level each
4
year, the change must, in no small measure, reflect increasing self-sufficiency
in the Japanese automobile industry. Significant, too, is the persistent and
marked drop in sales of iron and steel semi-manufactures other than iron and
steel scrap -- from $49,218,000 in 1937 to $21,051,000 in 1938, and to $12,997,000
in l939. Decline in this item further bears witness to the recent development of
Japanese heavy indus tries, which is bound to result in a continuing drop in her
imports of finished and semi-finished heavy manufactures.
Because of the oil surpluses built up particularly during l938, and also be-
cause of the compelling necessity of conserving foreign exchange, Japan in 1939
pared down considerably her petroleum purchases abroad. Last year's shipments
to Japan of petroleum and its products valued at $71, 220, 000, while only slightly
below the 1937 figure of $71, 599, 000, were 12 per cent below the 1938 purchases,
5
valued at $81,035,000.
Of great import is the further gain in l939 of shipments of metal-working
machinery over the unprecedented 1evel of 1938. The 1939 figure was $38, 203, 000,
compared with $36,449, 000 1n 1938 and $17,579,000 1n 1937. These hoavy orders by

Japan reflect her backwardness in the all-important machine tool industry, common-
ly regarded as the technological foundation of modern industrial and military
power, and underscore Nippon's dependence upon foreign sources for most types of
specialized machinery equipment which are absolutely necessary for the country's
program of armament and production expansion.
Broad and impressive gains were made in shipments of most metallic materials,
thus indicating Japan's aggravated deficiency in these war essentials. Sales of
scrap iron and steel to Japan rose to $35,808, 000 in 1939 after dropping from
61
44,982,000 in 1937 to 25,432,000 1n 1938.

valued at $6,553,000 1n 1937, and at $6,592,000 in 1938, amounted to $8,040,000

in 1939.

to $6,049,000 1n 1939.

As for the other changee in shipments, it is important to note that ores
other than iron ore, consisting predominantly of manganese ore, an essential in
steel mamufacture, increased in 1939 over 1938 by 38.8 per cent or $903,256.
Mica and asbestos advanced by 84.6 per cent or $671,000 and 48.7 per cent or
$652,000, respectively. On the other hand, h1des and skins, and leather, be-
cause of their relatively less urgent character from the standpoint of Japanese
war economy, both decreased furthor in l939 after dropping considerably in value
frum 1937 to 1938. The percentage deoline for 1939, compared with 1938, was 6.6
per cent for hides and skins, and 2l.3 per cent for leather. Shipments of in-
ternal combustion engines also were less in l939 than in either of the two pre-
vious years. The downward change from 1938 amounted to 44.4 per cent or $737,000.
Finished arms and ammunition, always an insignificant item, showed an increase
over 1938 of 30.7 per cent or $214,000.
II. Sources of Supply
Japan has been dependent upon at least twenty-eight foreign countries or
colonial dependencies, covering a far-flung area, for her supply of war materials.
However, seven of these, headed by the United States, accounted for the bulk of
the exports by contributing over 90 per cent of the total value of such shipments
9
in 1939, as indicated in Table III below. From the United States alone, Japan
has for three successive years received about 55-56 per cent of her supply of
war materials. Significant shares were contributed also by British Malay,
Canada, and Netherland India with 8.7 per cent, 8.5 per cent, and 8.3 per cent of
the total, respectively. Germany, British India and Italy shared to a lesser
degree in the total with 4.3 per cent, 3.3 per cent, and l.3 per cent, respectively.
The European war has greatly affected the export policies of countries which
have provided Japan with essential war materials in the past. Supplies from some
9
This does not mean thai smaller-sized contributions were unimportant; indis-
pensable commodities are supplied by countries which do not provide a signi-
ficant portion of the total value.

metal-working machinery, and copper. In addition, it was the most important sup-

In addition to curtailing generally her purchases of hides and skins and
leather, Japan bought relatively smaller quantities of these 1tems from the United
States than formerly. This country still supplies almost one-fourth of the total
hides and skins exported to Japan but a negligible portion of her decreasing leather
imports. The value of 1939 shipments of hides and skins declined 33.0 per cent
below 1938, and 34.0 per cent below 1937. It is interesting to note that scrap
rubber shipments from the U. S., though a relatively small item in the total, has
been rising markedly. The increase in 1939 over 1938 exceeds 200 per cent, while
the gain over 1937 is almost 350 per cent. Arms and ammunition, never an impor-
tant item, except, perhaps, in 1938, when the U. S. furnished 14.4 per cent of
the total, dropped to almost zero in 1939.
War materials shipments, as a group, have constituted an inoreasing proportion
of the total exports from the United States to Japan. In 1937, the total value
of all exports was $288,588,000, while war materials were valued at $168,383,000,
or 58.0 per cent of the total. In 1938, the total was $239,662,000 and war ma-
terials, $158,856,000 or 66.3 per cent. In 1939, the total amounted to
231,405,000 of which 67.0 per cent or $155,078,000 was represented by war ma-
terials.
Canada
Canada is an outs tanding source of supply for most of the non-ferrous metals.
In 1939 she contr1buted 90.5 per cent, or $8,443,000 of ths niokel exported to
Japan; 70.9 per cent, or $7,495, 000 of the aluminum; 40.3 per cent, or $2,566, 000
of the lead; and about 7.5 per cent or $2,314,000 of the copper. All of the
asbestos imported by Japan was from Canada, and rolatively small amounts of mica
and hides and skins also originated there.
In 1939, exports of Canadian war materials to Japan reached a total value
of 24,865,000, as aga1nst $18, 375,000 the previous year. Whereas in 1938, the
ratio of war material exports to all exports from Canada to Japan was 89 per cent,
in 1939 1t was 1ncreased to 92 per cent.

-10-
South and Bast Asia and Oceania
Because of the vast resources and the geographical proximity of South and
East Asia and Oceanic countries, Japan has long held a more than ordinary inter-
est in this area. The violent rupture of the status quo in Burope has had
repercussions in the Pacific, and Japan is seizing every opportunity to press
vigorously towerd including these countries within her expanding "new order".
It is of interest to indicate the extent to whioh the resources of this region
have been utilized in the past in equipping Japan's war machine.
For the purpose of this survey, South and East Asia and Oceania includes
British Malay, Netherland India, British India, China, the Philippines, Australia,
Hong Kong, French Indo-China, and French New Caledonia. Together they contributed
59.0 per cent of seven important items in Japan's war materials trade, namely,
petroleum, rubber, ores, tin, lead, zinc, and mioa, as can be seen in Table
VI.
This region supplied 33.5 per cent of the petroleum imported by Japan in
1939; 59.0 per cent of the rubber; 25.9 per cent of the lead; 20.5 per cent of
the zinc; 94.8 per cent of the mica; all of the iron ore and other important ores
(with the exception of some copper ore from Chile); and all of the tin.

While, on the whole, the European countries lent less assistance to Japan
in 1939 than in 1938, they still constituted an important source, particularly
for heavy mamufactures. Automobiles, parts and accessories exported by Germany
and Italy to Japan in 1939 were valued at $300,000 and 1,800,000, or 3.3 per
cent and 19.8 per cent of the total respectively. Metal-working machinery from
this area included shipmonts valued at $9, 200,000, or 24.1 per cent of the total,
from Germany; $1,100,000 or 2.9 per cent from the United Kingdom; and $1,090,089
or 2.9 per cent from Switzerland. Germany's supply of 34.7 per cent of the en-
tire export of internal combustion engines represented a value of $320,000; a
small amount was also contributed by Italy. Again Germany enters the picture as
an important supplier of aircraft and parts, valued in 1939 at $2,100,000, or
36.6 per cent of the total shipments of this item to Japan. Germany and Italy
together furnished all but a negligible portion of the arms and ammunition ship-
ped to Japan. Shipments from Italy amounted to $840,000, and from Germany,
70,000.

and steel semi-manufac tures from Sweden (estimated to be about 18 per cent of
the total 1n 1939), the United Kingdom, Belgium and Luxemburg, France, and Ger-

Japan's sole source for this metal).

percentages for these years being 22.1, 20.3 and 21.9. The Netherlands group,
Netherlands proper and Netherland India, accounted for 8.3 per cent in 1939, 8.7
per cent in 1938, and 9.4 per cent in 1937. The French group of France, French
Indo-China, and French New Caledonia, exported an insignificant portion of the
total in each of the three years, while the "Axis" group, which in l939, included
former Czechoslovakia and Austria, as well as Germany and Italy, decreased their
share from 8.2 per cent in 1938, to 5.6 per cent in 1939, after gaining from 3.4
per cent in 1937. The U. S. S. R., it should be observed, has adhered to a con-
sistent policy of non-participation in the Japanese campaign in China. No ship-
ments of war materials from the Soviet Union have reached Japan at any time during
the three years.

JAPAN'S PROCUREMENT PROBLEM AND THE STRATEGIC
POSITION OF THE UNITED STATES
Japan belongs to the group of economically weak military States. Besides
being handicapped by an inadequate heavy-industry base, she suffers from a deficienoy
of a wide range of articles and materials which are absolutely necessary to the
prosecution of modern warfare [see chart on following page]. To cover these short-
ages Japan must depend upon imports from abroad. Prior to the outbreak of European
hostilities the task was one of providing the necessary foreign exchange with
which to purchase the war essentials. Now another serious problem is confronting
the Nippon Empire, - the problem of maintaining her vital imports as the channels
of supply are being cut off.
Japan is faced, in consequence of the current war in Europe, with a virtually
complete stoppage of many lines of strategic supplies formerly received from the
European countries. These include aircraft, machine tools, automotive equipment,
special steel, and other heavy manufactures, as well as alumimm, nickel, zinc,
and mercury. She also finds it increasingly diffioult to obtain from the British
Empire a great variety of war necessities, principally nickel, tin, alumimum,
lead, zino, asbestos, mica, iron and manganese ores, petroleum, copper, and hides.
Because of urgent defense useds, practically all exportation of metals and
minerals from the British Empire is subject to a tightening license system. Sales
to foreign nations of Canadian scrap and Aus tralian iron ore are ac tually prohibit-
ed, while the bulk of the copper and alumimum output of Canadian companies, for
1
instance, has been pre-empted by the British Government.
Of the greatest concern to the Japanese is the threatened cessation of the
flow of war sinews from the United States. Japan has for three successive years
obtained in this country about 55-56 per cent of her total imports of war mater-
ials, and all evidence indicates that since the European war her dependence upon
output and 90 per cent of exportable Canadian aluminum.

Economic Research, based upon authoritative sources in the field, including G. A.
Rousch, Strategic Mineral Supplies, 1939, pp. 454-469; and John W. Finch and James W.

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.98)
-15-
American supplies has been greatly increased. Contimmed access to American mater-
ials and equipment is, therefore, absolutely necessary for maintaining the Japan-
ese war machine at a leval of effectiveness that is required by the military ad-
ventures abroad.
The Government of the United States has taken three major steps directly to-
ward, or indirectly having the effect of, restricting or stopping exports to
Japan which are essential for war purposes. On June 1l, 1938, the State Depart-
ment, without naming Japan, 1mposed a "moral embargo" on "the exports of aircraft
armements, engines, parts, accessories, aerial bombs and torpedoes to countries,
the armed forces of which were engaged in the bombing of civilian populations by
2/
air."
The "moral embargo" list was extended on December 15, 1939, obviously
not without reference also to countries other than Japan, to include molybdemm
and alumimum. Five days later, on December 20, the coverage was further broadened
to embrace equipment and information required for the production of high quality
aviation gasoline.
The second major step taken by the United States Government was in connection
with the abrogation of the l9ll American-Japanese trade treaty, which was termin-
ated in January, l940. This action removed the legal obstacles to levying a
mandatory embargo against Japan.
The third and most important action relates to President Roosevelt's pro-
clamation of July 2, l940, establishing regulations for control of exports of
munitions, materials and machinery essential to national defenee. The proclama-
tion listed a long series of materials and articles to be subject to export
3
license. Included in this list are seven items whioh figure in U. S. war-material
export trade with Japan. These are in the order of importance: metal-working
macainery; ferro-alloys (molybdemum and tungsten); aircraft, parts, equipment and
2
See Third Annual Report of the National Munitions Control Board, p. 80. (U. S.
Government Printing Office)
3
For full details, see Te New York Times, July 2, 1940, p. 1l.

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.98)
-16-
4
accessories; alumimm; hides; scrap rubber; arms and ammunition.
A significant feature of this Administrative action is that it provides, as
occasion arises, extension of the list of things requiring export control. By a
subsequent proclamation of July 26, l940, the President added to the list three
more items, as follows: (l) Petroleum protucts, defined as aviation motor fuel
and aviation lubricating oil; (2) Iron and steel scrap, defined as No. 1 heavy
5
melting scrap; and (3) Tetraethyl lead (an ingredient used in raising the octane
number of gasoline). It is to be noted that while there are no official data
showing shipments of tetraethyl lead to Japan, U. S. exports of the first two
items to that country have boen both heavy and strategically important.
As a result of the two proclamations, approximately 26 per cent of the war
6
materials exported from the United States to Japan are now subject to license.
Only in the case of aviation motor fuel, representing 3 per cent of the total of
war material shipments, has the export control assumed the form of an embargo.
The license system, it should be noted, is a discretionary form of export regula-
tion. It remains to be seen to what extent it is going to be applied in curtail-
ing shipments to Japen of those items now so regulated.
The all-important fact, however, is that the American Administration, by
virtue of the authority vested in the President by the national defense act of
July 2, l940, possesses the power, which it did not have before, to clamp down
at any time a complete export ban on the war mater:als now being shipped to Japan.
This at once makes Nippon's procurement of vital supplies from the United States
dangerously precarious, and sharply raises the question as to what would happen
to the Japanese war machine in the event of a full-fledged American anbargo.
4 In the case of metal-working machinery, about 90 per cent of the shipments are
subjeot to license. There are also several negligible itams such as tin, mer-
cury, and mica, which have not been included in our statistical compilations
of U. S. war-material export trade with Japan.
5
Representing approximately 20 per cent of the total U. S. shipments of iron and
steel sorap to Japan.
6
This percentage and the one following have been calculated on the basis of
figures for U. S. war material shipments to Japan in 1939.

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.98)
-17-
Japan depends on this country for all of the four great war essentials she
imports, namaly, petroleum and its products, metal-working machinery, iron and
steel scrap, and copper. In the case of iron and steel scrap and metal-working
machinery, the dependence is virtually complete in the sense that there is no
other alternative source available to Japan. Fully a third of the entire pro-
duction of the Japanese steel industry - the backbone of armament and munition
7
mamufaoture - is based on American scrap.
As regards American machine tools,
they are absolutely indispensable to Japan in connection with the execution of her
wartime industrial and armament program, partioularly in view of the elimination of
8
Germany as a supply market.
The "machine tool manufac ture", stated the Oriental
Economist, "has been a very backward section of the Japanese machinery industry
and the country has long been dependent on foreign sources for nearly all of its
requirements in the high class precision tools." U. S. shipments of metal-working
machinery to Japan has advanced to $15, 523, 000 during the first six months of
1940 from $14,569,000 during the similar period last year. There is no doubt
that if the American Government should decide to prohibit entirely the exportation
of these two strategic items to Japan, the blow would be singularly heavy to her
whole war economy. To date, the Administration has contented itself with the
licensing control covering shipments to Japan of about 90 per-cent of the metal-working
machinery and only 20 per cent of the scrap iron and steel, representing No. 1
heavy melting scrap. No definite information is as yet available as to whether,
and to what extent, export licenses for these shipments have been refused.
Over 90 per cent of Nippon's current demands for oil, estimated to be in the
neighborhood of 42 million barrels a year, must be met by supplies from abroad.
The United States furnishes Japan two-thirds of her total oil imports, shipments
consisting mainly of crude petroleum and aleo of gas and fuel oil, residual fuel
7

to Japan, which amounted to 2,026,764 metric tons, to last year's Japanese
steel production estimated at around 6 million metric tons. For a detailed
discussion on Japan's virtually complete dependence on American scrap iron
and steel, see Chinese Council For Economic Research, Memorandum on Japan's
Dependence on American Sorap, June, 1940.

oil and, what is of great importance, gasoline for aviation purposes. As previously
mentioned, the American Government has placed exports of aviation gasoline and
aviation lubricating oil under licensing control, thus affecting about 14 per cent
of the total U. S. petroleum export trade with the Ialand Empire. In the case of
aviation gasoline, licenses are to be grented only for shipments destined for
countries in the Western Hemisphere, which is tantamount to an embargo as far as
sales to Japan are concerned. Serious repercussions on Japan would follow a ban
by the United States on all oil exports to that country, particularly if the
action should be taken in collaboration with the British Government. This is
9
because of the two dominant factors governing the situation. In the first place,
sources of petroleum at present accessible to Japan, except in the case of
10/
Mexico, are practically all controlled by Anglo-American interests.
Secondly,
it is very unlikely that the Netherlands East Indies, presumably a logical place
for Japan to turn for replacement of American oil, would do anything to nullify
the Anglo-American parallel action.
11
Japan is about 6C per cent deficient in copper, a non-ferrous metal, which
in the language of an authoritative Tokyo journal, "is absolutely essential to
12/
the munition indus tries".
The shortage has been covered by imports, over 90 per
cent of which are furnished by the United States. In the event of a stoppage of
supplies from this country, Japan may seek their replacement in Chile, the world's

second largest producer of oopper. However, since Chilean copper production is
practically all under the control of American interests, namely, the Anaconda
Copper Mining Company and the Kennecott Copper Corporation, the success of Japan's
efforts in this direction would be subject to uncertainties.
In addition to the foregoing four great essentials, Japan depends on the
United States for a whole range of special steel semi-manufactures, and for auto-
motive equipment as well as a considerable amount of lead. The exports of all
these commodities from the United States are not at the present time subject to
licensing control. Under circumstances now prevailing, no alternative sources
of supply exist for Japan in regard to both automotive equipment and special
steel materials. The Japanese lack the facilities for the fashioning of special
steel. Neither have they attained self-sufficiency with respect to motor trucks,
automobile parts, engines and accessories, despite the progress recently made in
their automobile indus try.

The data have been compiled from the official trade returns of the various exporting
countries as well as from other official sources. This statistical compilation must
of necessity have been based upon export figures, due to the fact that the Japanese
Government, since August, 1937, has discontinued the 1ssuance of data on "essential
imports". However, the Monthly return of the Foreign Trade of Japan continues the
publication of import figures for hides and skins, leather and rubber, and these
figures have been used in the tabulation.
