case of leather and rubber export figures hate been employed for shipments from the
United States. The preliminary figure of Philippine exports of hides and skins to
Japan for 1937 has been revised upward from $61,919 to $130,172.

Figures prepared by the Dutch authorities in respect of oil exports from Netherland
India to Japan are most unreliable on account of the many transshipment points which
obscure the final destination beyond recognition. It has been estimated by us that
about 7,100,000 bb1. of 011 ($16,800,000) 1n 1939, 8,627,000 bb1. ($19,239,000) 1n
1938 and 8,297,000 bb1. ($16,842,000) in 1937, wh1ch were offic1ally reported as ex-
ports from Netherland India to Poeloe Samboe and Poeloe Bintan (transshipment points),
finally went to Japan. Figures given here have been arrived et by adding these
transshipments to the oil exports to Japan as shown in the official trade returns
of Netherland India.

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
THE AMERICAN LEGION
Mirando Post No. 385
Mirando City, Texas
Sept. 20, 1940
Federal Bureau of Investigation,
San Antonio, Texas
Gentlemen:
It 1s our impression down in this part of the State
that scrap iron must not be sold to Japan. It is also
our opinion that anyone who does so 1s not a bit patriotic
in view of the questions now up between this country and
Japan.
Mirando City 1s a little o1l town on the Texas Mexican
Railway. Being so situated we have ample opportunity to
observe what passes to and fro on this little railway.
Among the things Being shipped via the Tex Mex 1s consider
able BGRAP IRON. Enroute over said line, originating in
Corpus Christi and destined for Laredo, Texas, are from
two to six carloads of scrap EVERY DAY. Our information ts
that the scrap 1s sold "to Mexico". From Mexico it is
then shipped to Japan.
It 1s reported that same 1s being handled by a well
known sorap 1ron firm by the name of Federal Export Company.
To Corpus shipments are being made by other sorap
dealers, thence from Corpus to Laredo; thence from Laredo
to Mexico; thence via Vera Cruz to Japan.
It would appear to us that ALL SCRAP DEALERS SHOULD
BE INVESTIGATED (with a capital I (eve).
We submit the above in the best spirit, and if our
information 1s correct, and you find it so, we hope that
something will be done shortly to pour it on their sore
back.
Very truly yours,
HERBERT F. DANMIER
Past Commander, Mirando Post No.385,Amer.Leg1on
E. T. HARVEY
Present Post Commander M1rando Post No. 385, Amer. Legion
cc: Hon. Milton West, Washington, D. C.
cc: Hon. Richard M. Kleberg, Washington, D. C.

WITTE ENGINE WORKS
Kansas City, Mo. U.S.A.
September 20, 1940
Honorable Arthur Capper
U.S. Senator
Senate Building
Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator:
We have written you twice before in reference to a
matter that we feel it necessary to bring up again, and
more urgently than ever.
white it is true that the President is now permitted
to license the shipment of No.1 heavy scrap iron and steel
to foreign countries, heavy machinery scrap is only a small
portion of the scrap iron and steel that is leaving this
country and causing a very serlous situation with all foundry-
men.
I am afraid the neglect of Congress to embargo ship-
ments of all iron and steel scrap, unless perhaps to England,
1s becoming a serious matter--and the government 1s really
paying the bill.
Recently, and for the second time this year, we were
awarded a sizable order for Diesel electric units for sub-
marine and mine base lighting. However, our requirements
are 1nfinitesimal as compared to large concerns making or
building ships, tanks, artillery, structural steel for new
factories, etc. etc.
I can't imagine for a moment why this country should
continue to allow scrap iron and steel of any sort whatsoever
to be shipped to foreign countries, leaving us to pay $17 or
$18 per ton, when 3 to 6 months ago 1t was between $10 and
$12.
It 1s a great mistake to assume that a certain class of
scrap 1ron and steel 1s needed. Almost anything can be re-
melted and built up with doctor iron so as to produce the
proper casting.
I hope, dear Senator, thet you will give this matter
more than verfunctory acknowledgement and that you will

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
help every iron and steel user in this country, many of
whom are doing government work.
With kindeet regards, I am
Yours very truly
WITTE ENGINE WORKS
E.H.WITTE, PRESIDENT

The following appeared under the above heading in
the August 15th issue of Oil and Gas, page 40:
"The Korean Oil Co., Ltd. (Chosen Sekiyu K.K.), a
joint enterprise of the Nippon Nitrogen Fertilizer Co.
and Nippon Petroleum Co., will shortly begin construc-
tion on a plant to produce isooctane for use as aviation
fuel.
894.24/1880
Japan has already one isooctane plant, which was
built under U.O.P. license, and a second plant, also a
Universal design, is nearing completion.
Both plants
are located at Kawasaki, between Tokyo and Yokohama.
Since Japan is unable to obtain American blue-
prints and engineering assistance under the existing
moral embargo" ruling, oil men here sneculate on the
process to be used by Korean Oil, which has not been
disclosed by the firm.
The Dai Nippon Salt Industrial Co., which is
producing a score of chemicals from sea water, has
finished work on a pilot plant producing ethylene di-
bromide for use as entiknock agent in conjunction with
tetraethyl lead.
The plant is located at Kamata, near
Tokyo.
Production of tetraethyl lead will be taken up
shortly by the Nippon Soda Co. and the Hodogaya Chemical
Co. Two of the three patents reserving the use of the
lead compound to Ethyl Gasoline Cor. expired last year."

Mt. Enterprise, Texas.
September 30, 1940.
Hon. Lindley Beckworth,
Washington, D.C.
My dear Congressman:
Just a line to try to get to a few things very
definite.
For a good long time the Japanese Government has been
rearing up at certain statements this Government has made
to them. Certain treaties have been signed with them.
They have now signed an agreement with Germany and Italy,
another slap in the face of the people of the United States
and our Government. All of these things you are much bet-
ter informed than I, but I do know a little about it.
Now the question 1s:
Why has the Government continued to sell to Japan such
abundance of Scrap Iron to use against China? You say an
Embargo 1s placed areinst this now. Alright.
Why does the Government continue to sell them Pig Iron,
Petroleum geeoline, oils etc. for the purpose of waging war
on China, and now hoarding up a great sup: ly of these ma-
terials so essential in war, when it looks as if they are
doing everything possible to intimidate our Government.
This is a puzzle.
The people of this country are anxious to know why
such 1s being permitted. I think they have a right to
know why the Government proteet to Japan for waging war on
China, yet turning around and at the same time sell to Japan
the essentials for weging such a war. We have greetly
bungled thinge, end now heve gotten beyond the point where
protest will do no good. We have reached the place where
we are toing to stand up to some of the protests or back down
and let another nation sey we are too engrossed in making
money and enjoying an easy life to do anything about it.
Kindly pass the word alon for an explanation of such
a policy.
I have been wanting to write you about this for a long
time, but have thought may be the protests would avail,
but we see that they have not, nelther will avail in deal-
ing with Japen, nor any other of the warring natione now.

Cordially yours,

For some time, and especially in the light of recent

Japan has been far from strong. Why is it that some ac-

My dear Mr. Hull:

on a subject that lies very close to my heart. Why is it that this
country persists in furnishing Japan with munitions of war to oppress
and destroy China, when our own interests are so largely bound up with
China and when Japan is taking anything but a friendly attitude toward
894.24
our own country. Our people are almost unanimous in the desire to give
all the help they can to Chine and as little as possible to Japan. I
should warmly appreciate an explanati n of what seems to ne a very
remarkable lack of wise action on the part of our government.
With best wishes, I am

MR WELLEN
：
PAH.
Mr. Halle of the Universal Oil Products Company and
394.24/108
his assistant, Mr. Healy, called on me this morning. They
said that they had been receiving almost daily calls from
Mr. Saneyoshi of the Japan Gasoline Company and that they
are desirous of knowing whether or not the Department will
permit them to carry out any of the provisions of their
5
894.24
1938 contract with that company. They said that
Mr. Saneyoshi 1s especially desirous of obtaining informa-
tion in regard to simple or non-selective polymerization
and in regard to the hydrogenation process.
In reply I informed Mr. Halle and Mr. Healy in con-
fidence that 1t 1s my belief that a way has been found to
provide a legal basis for the moral embargo on plans, plants,
and technical information for tne production of aviation
gasoline and that if the proposeu procedure is approved,
7
licenses will scon be reauired for the export of any
3
technical or descriptive information useful in the
production

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
production of any of the aviation motor fuel defined in the
President's regulations of July 26. I emphasized the fact
that I had no way of knowing whether or not this procedure
would be approved and said that I believed that a definite
decision would be reached one way or another within a few
days. I added that I could make no reply to their questions
until this matter of general policy is settled and pointed
out that, if the proposed procedure is adopted, licenses
will be required for the export to Japan of the technical
information covered by their 1938 contract with the Japan
Gasoline Company. Whether or not applications for such
licenses would be granted would be a matter to be decided
by the Administrator of Export Control after consultation
with all the interested Departments and agencies.
Mr. Halle said that in view of this situation he would
make no commitments to the Japan Gasoline Company at the
present time. He said that it 1s his belief that
Mr. Saneyoshi intends to return to Japan about September 20,
but that he will come back to the United States in a few
months' time. Mr. Halle believes that the Japan Gasoline
Company's suit against the Universal Oil Products Company
for v1olation of the 1938 contract will be held over until
Mr. Saneyoshi's return.
Mr. Healy

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.96)
-3
Mr. Healy pointed out that there are two processes
covered by the l938 contract which do not relate to avia-
tion motor fuel. One is a process for the manufacture of
synthetic rubber and the other a process for the manufacture
of toluene. Mr. Healy said that Mr. Saneyoshi has no inter-
est in the former process since Japan is in a position to
obtain ample supplies of crude rubber. Mr. Halle declared
that he would not even ask the Department its views on
the subject of toluene since he was certain that we would
not wish him to supply the Japanese with a process for the
manufacture of a material eo unmistakably intended for war
use.

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE
THE WHITE HOUSE
FOR THE PRESS
SEPTEMBER 26, 1940
CONTROL OF IRON AND STEEL SCRAP EXPORTS
The President has approved the early establishment
of additional controls of the exportation of iron and steel
scrap with a view to conserving the available supply to
meet the rapidly expanding pocuirements of the defense
program in this country.
Effective October 15, 1940, all outstanding balances
of licenses which have been granted pursuant to the exist-
ing regulations of July 26, 1940, for the exportation of
894.24/1085
No. 1 heavy melting steel sorap will be revoked. On
October 16, 1940, the exocrtation of all grades of iron
and steol scrap will be placed under the licensing system.
Under the new regulations which will be made effective
on October 16, 1940, licenses will be issued to permit
shipments to the countries of the Western Hemisphere and
Great Britain only.

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.99)
LEO ALEXANDER, M. D.
Boston City Hospital
818 Harrison Avenue
Boston, Mass.
September 30, 1940.
Mr. George J. Bates,
House Office Building,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Representatives Bates:
I should like to express my great concern regarding
our position with Japan. Japan has now come out openly
as our enemy and we atill have not taken measures to
stop supplying her with war materials. The embargo of
scrap iron alone 1s insufficient and I feel that gasoline
and all other raw materials should be included in the em-
bargo. Furthermore, imports from Japan should be stopped
completely. The exolusion of Japanese silk from the
American market would not only prove an effective blow
against the economy of our deolared enemy but in addition
1t would strengthen our home industry of nylon and rayon.
We have aided and abetted our enemy Japan for far
too long a period.
Sincerely yours,
LEO ALEXANDER, M. D.
LA/RB

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.97)
PARAPHRASE OF TELEGBAK
To:
Secretary of State.
From: Madrid (unsigned).
Dated: October 7, 1940.
Roo'd: Ootober 8, 1940, 12:15 a.m.
Number: 536.
I am told by the Naval Attaché that he received
today a lengthy unannounced visit from the Japanese
Naval Attaché in Madrid, who said that he was calling
on instructions from his Minister to state that "Japan
must live" and that if the United States continued the
embargo measures which it was applying or was about to
apply on shipments of certain essential materials,
espeoially gasoline, to Japan, war would be the result.
EV.

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.98)
X
REOEIVED
537
JUN 2 6 1941
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
UIVISION OF
DTH
June 24, 1941
Herewith two nemoranda prepared by
officers of FE commenting upon probable Japa-
nese reactions to the outbreak of war between
Germany and Russia. The first memorandum, pre-
pared by Mr. Schmidt, and concurred in by the
senior officers of FE, represents what I
believe to be the most probable Japanese
reactions. The second memorandum, prepared by
Mr. Langdon, a senior Japanese language officer
who has just returned to the United States from
some five years service at Mukden followed by
a short assignment at the Tokyo Embassy, sets
forth factors which may operate to discourage
embarkation by Japan upon a military attack
upon Siberia. While I believe that the factors
listed by Mr. Langdom warrant consideration,
they represent in my opinion factors bearing
only upon one side of the question.
The two memoranda contain tentative and
preliminary expressions of view which, although
they may be changed in the light of further
analysis and further developments, I believe
you will find of interest. The viewpoints ex-
pressed in both memoranda point to the
probability that there is likely to ensue in
Japan a period of uncertainty and of extreme
difficulty in deciding upon a future course.
Copies to Mr. Hornbeck and Mr. Atherton.
FE:MMH:HNS
